## Reasoning

### Mission Analysis
- **Goal**: MINIMISE score
- Items from one half decrease score by 1 (GOOD), items from the other half increase by 1 (BAD)
- We need robots to collect from the GOOD half (let's say BLUE wall side) and avoid the BAD half (GREEN wall side)
- Robots perceive: floor color (BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=middle), wall colors (RED=nest side, BLUE/GREEN=source halves)

### Key Strategy
- Robots should go to the BLACK floor (source area)
- Prefer the BLUE-walled half (GoToColor with color matching BLUE=3 or check colors)
- Return to WHITE floor (nest) to deliver
- Avoid GREEN half

### Color codes
- Looking at the format: `--clr<I>` one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}
- Colors for robot LEDs: 0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan
- For `--l<I>x<K>` (camera): 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

### Champion Analysis
The champion (-1.51) uses:
- State 0: Exploration with transitions on low/high neighbors
- State 1: Attraction, transitions on WHITE floor
- State 2: Repulsion, transitions on BLACK floor and high neighbors
- State 3: GoAwayColor (yellow=4), transitions on WHITE floor, yellow perception, high neighbors

### Design Plan

**Survival**: Copy champion exactly.

**Exploitation** (small edits to champion): Adjust probabilities, thresholds, gains by ~10%.

**Exploration**: New topologies focusing on:
1. GoToColor BLUE (3) to find good items, GoAwayColor GREEN (2) to avoid bad items
2. Simple 3-state: explore → go to source → return to nest
3. 2-state machines for simplicity
4. Machines that use floor color transitions more aggressively

Wait, I need to be more careful with transition indexing. Let me re-examine the format.

With `--n<I> T`, there are T transitions from state I, indexed K=0..T-1.
`--n<I>x<K> D` where D is the index among OTHER states.

For 3 states (0,1,2), leaving state 0: other states are [1,2], so D=0→state1, D=1→state2.
For state 1: other states are [0,2], D=0→state0, D=1→state2.
For state 2: other states are [0,1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1.

Let me redo the exploration machines carefully:

**Exploration 1**: 3-state: explore → GoToColor(BLUE=3) for source → GoToColor(RED=1) for nest

- State 1: GoToColor BLUE (approach blue wall = good half), on WHITE go to state 2
- State 2: GoToColor RED (approach red wall = nest), on BLACK go to state 0

**Exploration 2**: 3-state: explore → GoAwayColor(GREEN=2) avoid bad half → GoToColor(RED=1) return

**Exploration 3**: 2-state simple forager

**Exploration 4**: 4-state with GoToColor BLUE and GoAwayColor GREEN

**Exploration D**: 4-state targeted foraging
- S0: Exploration, BLACK→S1, WHITE→S3
- S1: GoToColor BLUE (seek good half), BLACK→S2, WHITE→S3, GRAY→S0
- S2: Stop (wait at source for item pickup), random→S3
- S3: GoToColor RED (return to nest), WHITE→S0, BLACK→S1

Wait S2 stop doesn't make much sense since item pickup is automatic. Let me rethink.

Actually picking up items is automatic when entering the half. So the key is:
1. Go to BLACK floor (source area)
2. Specifically go toward BLUE wall (good half)
3. Return to WHITE floor (nest) to deliver

**Exploration D**: 4-state
- S0: Exploration, BLACK→S1
- S1: GoToColor BLUE, WHITE→S2, GRAY→S0, random(low)→S3
- S2: GoToColor RED (return), WHITE→S0, BLACK→S1
- S3: GoAwayColor GREEN, BLACK→S1, WHITE→S2

For 4 states:
- Leaving state 0: others [1,2,3] → D=0=S1, D=1=S2, D=2=S3
- Leaving state 1: others [0,2,3] → D=0=S0, D=1=S2, D=2=S3
- Leaving state 2: others [0,1,3] → D=0=S0, D=1=S1, D=2=S3
- Leaving state 3: others [0,1,2] → D=0=S0, D=1=S1, D=2=S2

FSM: --nstates 4 --s0 0 --rwm0 50 --cle0 5 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.8 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --cle1 6 --clr1 3 --n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.9 --n1x1 2 --c1x1 1 --p1x1 0.5 --s2 8 --vel2 1.0 --cle2 4 --clr2 1 --n2 2 --n2x0 0 --c2x0 2 --p2x0 0.85 --n2x1 1 --c2x1 0 --p2x1 0.6 --s3 9 --vel3 1.0 --cle3 0 --clr3 2 --n3 2 --n3x0 1 --c3x0 0 --

## Reasoning

### Analysis of Champion (-1.78)
The new champion uses:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=40), transitions on low neighbors (C=4) → state 2, high neighbors (C=3) → state 2
- State 1: Attraction (att=1.4561), transition on WHITE floor → state 2
- State 2: Repulsion (rep=2.137), transitions on BLACK floor → state 2 (self? no...), high neighbors → state 2, BLACK floor → state 2
- State 3: GoAwayColor GREEN (clr=2), transitions on WHITE → state 2, perceive GREEN (C=7,l=2) → state 1, high neighbors → state 2

Wait, let me re-read: destinations are indexed among OTHER states. With 4 states, leaving state 0: others=[1,2,3], D=0→S1, D=1→S2, D=2→S3. 

So state 0's transitions: D=2→S3, D=2→S3 (both go to S3). State 2's transitions all go to D=2→S3 as well. State 3's transitions: D=2→S3? No, D=2 from state 3 = S2 (others=[0,1,2]).

Let me carefully re-read the champion:
- S0: D=2→S3, D=2→S3 (both transitions go to S3)
- S1: D=2→S3 (transition goes to S3)  
- S2: D=2→S3, D=2→S3, D=2→S3 (all go to S3)
- S3: D=2→S2, D=1→S1, D=2→S2

So the flow is: everything goes to S3 (GoAwayColor GREEN), from S3 → S2 (Repulsion) or S1 (Attraction). This makes sense - avoid GREEN, then repulse/attract as needed.

### Strategy
- The champion avoids GREEN (bad half) and uses repulsion/attraction for navigation
- Key insight: clr=2 in GoAwayColor means avoiding GREEN walls → steering toward BLUE side

### Plan
1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Small tweaks to champion parameters
3. **Exploration**: New topologies with GoToColor BLUE (clr=3) for active targeting

## Reasoning

### Champion Analysis (-10.12)
The champion is a massive improvement! It uses:
- **S0**: Exploration (rwm=40), transitions: low neighbors→S3, high neighbors→S3 (both go to S3)
- **S1**: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3), transitions: WHITE→S3, GRAY→S0 (D=0 from S1 = S0)
- **S2**: Repulsion (rep=2.137), transitions: BLACK→S3, high neighbors→S3, BLACK→S3 (all to S3)
- **S3**: GoAwayColor GREEN (clr=2), transitions: WHITE→S3... 

Wait, let me re-read destinations carefully for 4 states:
- From S0: others=[1,2,3], D=0→S1, D=1→S2, D=2→S3
- From S1: others=[0,2,3], D=0→S0, D=1→S2, D=2→S3
- From S2: others=[0,1,3], D=0→S0, D=1→S1, D=2→S3
- From S3: others=[0,1,2], D=0→S0, D=1→S1, D=2→S2

Champion flow:
- S0: D=2→S3, D=2→S3 (both transitions go to S3=GoAwayGreen)
- S1 (GoToColor BLUE): D=2→S3(WHITE), D=0→S0(GRAY)
- S2 (Repulsion): D=2→S3(BLACK), D=2→S3(highN), D=2→S3(BLACK)
- S3 (GoAwayGreen): D=2→S2(WHITE), D=1→S1(perceiveGreen), D=2→S2(highN)

So: Start exploring → S3 (avoid green) → S2 (repulse to spread out) or S1 (go to BLUE when green seen) → S3 again. S1 (GoToColor BLUE) returns to S0 on GRAY or to S3 on WHITE.

This is very clever: robots avoid GREEN, go toward BLUE, return to nest (RED perceived?), use repulsion to spread.

### Strategy for Round 4
1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Tune parameters of champion - adjust rwm, att, rep gains, probabilities, thresholds
3. **Exploration**: Try variations with GoToColor RED (clr=1) as explicit nest-return, different neighbor thresholds

Key notes on errors: `--cle` must be {0,4,5,6}; all transitions need destination index `--n<I>x<K> D`.

## Reasoning

### Champion Analysis (-12.62)
The new champion uses:
- **S0**: GoAwayColor GREEN (clr=2), transitions: WHITE→S1(D=1), perceiveGreen→S2(D=2), highNeighbors→S2(D=2)
- **S1**: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3), transitions: WHITE→S0(D=0), GRAY→S2(D=2... wait)

Let me re-decode destinations carefully for 4 states:
- From S0: others=[1,2,3], D=0→S1, D=1→S2, D=2→S3
- From S1: others=[0,2,3], D=0→S0, D=1→S2, D=2→S3
- From S2: others=[0,1,3], D=0→S0, D=1→S1, D=2→S3
- From S3: others=[0,1,2], D=0→S0, D=1→S1, D=2→S2

Champion flow:
- **S0** (GoAwayGreen): WHITE(p=0.85)→S1(GoToBlue), perceiveGreen(p=0.7)→S2(Repulsion), highN(7,w=14)→S2(Repulsion)
- **S1** (GoToBlue): WHITE(p=0.8)→S0(GoAwayGreen), GRAY(p=0.6)→S3(Exploration)
- **S2** (Repulsion rep=2): BLACK(p=0.4)→S0(GoAwayGreen), highN(2,w=10)→S0(GoAwayGreen)
- **S3** (Exploration rwm=40): lowN(5,w=4)→S0(GoAwayGreen), highN(9,w=5)→S0(GoAwayGreen)

Logic: Robots explore/repulse, avoid green (bad half), go to blue (good half) when at source, return when on white. Smart!

### Error notes
- `--cle` must be {0,4,5,6} only - never use 1,2,3
- All transitions need `--n<I>x<K> D` destination

### Strategy
1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Tune parameters - adjust probabilities, thresholds, gains ~10%
3. **Exploration**: Different topologies building on the GoAwayGreen+GoToBlue pattern

## Reasoning

### Situation Assessment
The champion (-12.88) has held for 3 rounds now. We're stuck in a local optimum. I need to be more aggressive with exploration while maintaining the champion.

### Error Analysis (persistent!)
The errors keep appearing:
1. `--cle` values must ONLY be {0, 4, 5, 6} - never 1, 2, or 3
2. Every `--n<I>x<K>` MUST be followed by destination D before the condition

### Champion Structure Review
- S0 (GoAwayGreen, clr=2): 3 transitions → all point to S1 or S2
- S1 (GoToBlue, clr=3): 2 transitions → S0 or S3  
- S2 (Repulsion): 2 transitions → both S0
- S3 (Exploration): 2 transitions → both S0

The key loop: Explore(S3) → AvoidGreen(S0) → GoToBlue(S1) → Explore(S3) again if GRAY. Repulsion(S2) handles crowding.

### New Exploration Ideas
1. Replace Repulsion with Attraction to gather robots at source
2. Try GoToColor RED (clr=1) for explicit nest-seeking after pickup
3. Try perceive-BLUE (l=3) instead of perceive-GREEN (l=2) in S0
4. Vary the neighbor thresholds significantly
5. Try 2-state extreme simplicity
6. Make S1 transition on BLACK (confirmed at source) → return behavior

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
